    "content": "Candidate Diseases: \n[\"F22\"]\n\nInterview Transcript: \nDoctor: Hello, I’m Dr. ____. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess I'm here because... well, for months now I’ve been trying to get people to understand what's going on, and nobody is listening. It’s my husband’s family. They are quietly messing with our money and ruining our good name. \n\nLike, I’ll be at work—I’m a billing clerk over at the regional supply office—and these little billing errors just keep showing up on my accounts. Which is ridiculous because I've been doing this job since 2012 and I never make mistakes like that. Though I will say, they just switched our software to this new cloud system last fall, and the girl who came in to train us wore this perfume that gave me the absolute worst migraine, just smelling like cheap vanilla all day long, and the screens are so bright now... \n\nBut anyway, the errors. They show up out of nowhere, and then it spreads. Suddenly someone at church acts funny toward me, like they've heard things. Last Sunday, Mary Anne barely even looked at me during the potluck, just handed me a napkin and turned her back. And I know what people say, but no matter what records anyone pulls up to try and explain it away or blame the computer, I know it ties back to his family. They're behind all of it.\n\nDoctor: How certain are you that your husband’s family is behind these problems?\n\nPatient: I am absolutely certain. I mean, how could I not be? It's just so obvious when you look at the whole picture. Like I said, the billing errors at work... and then the church thing. Mary Anne has always been a little two-faced, honestly. Last year at the Christmas bazaar, she promised she'd bring three dozen of those little pecan tartlets, and she showed up with store-bought cookies. Store-bought! And she left the price tag on the bottom of the plastic tray. I saw it when I was helping clean up the kitchen. \n\nBut anyway, yes, I know it's them. My husband's family. They are doing this. It doesn't matter what anyone says or what kind of printouts they show me from the new system. I know they're messing with our money and trying to ruin my reputation. It all connects back to them, plain as day.\n\nDoctor: If someone showed you clear proof that your husband’s family was not involved, would you be willing to consider that you might be mistaken?\n\nPatient: Proof? I mean... what kind of proof would that even be? Because honestly, no matter what records anyone pulls up or tries to wave in my face, I know it ties back to them. I've been trying to get people to understand this for months now. They are quietly messing with our money and our good name, and all these little billing errors at work and people acting funny at church... it's them. I'm not mistaken. \n\nBut speaking of records, my husband's sister, Brenda... she keeps all her important paperwork in these old Nike shoeboxes out in her garage. I saw them when we went over for a barbecue last Memorial Day. Or maybe it was the Fourth of July? It was definitely summer because my husband burned his hand on the grill. I bought him these really nice, long tongs from Target specifically so he wouldn't do that, but he insisted on using the short metal ones we've had forever. He just never listens. And Brenda's garage is just a complete fire hazard with all that cardboard stacked up next to the lawnmower. I told her she should get a proper filing cabinet, they have them on sale at Office Depot all the time. \n\nBut anyway, no. I wouldn't consider that I'm wrong, because I know exactly what his family is doing to us.\n\nDoctor: Have you generally been suspicious and mistrustful of other people for as long as you can remember, even before these recent problems?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I've always been a very trusting person, honestly. I never had any reason to look over my shoulder before his family started all this. I used to trust everyone. \n\nLike my old neighbor, Mrs. Gable, I used to give her the spare key to feed our cat, Barnaby, whenever we went out of town. We went to Myrtle Beach back in... gosh, 2017? No, 2018, because that was the year my husband insisted on driving instead of flying, and the AC in the Honda went out halfway through North Carolina. We had to stop at this little diner, and I had the best slice of cherry pie I've ever eaten. The crust was just so flaky. I've tried making it at home with that store-bought Pillsbury dough, but it just doesn't bake up the same way, you know? My oven runs a little hot, I think. I told the landlord about it, but he just sent his son over to look at it, and that boy doesn't know a wrench from a screwdriver. He tracked mud all over my kitchen rug, too. It’s one of those nice washable ones from Ruggable, but still, it's the principle of the thing. \n\nBut anyway, what was I saying? Oh, right. No, I was never suspicious of people before. I was always perfectly friendly and got along with everyone until they started doing all this to us.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard or seen things that other people around you could not?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't. I mean, I don't hear voices or see ghosts or anything crazy like that. My hearing is actually perfectly fine. I had it checked last year when I went in for that sinus infection that just wouldn't quit. \n\nI was taking Sudafed for two weeks straight, and it made me so jittery I couldn't even drink my morning coffee. And I love my morning coffee. I usually get those Hazelnut K-cups, the ones by Donut Shop, but they were completely sold out at Kroger last week. I had to settle for the store brand, and let me tell you, it tastes like hot dirt. My husband said he couldn't taste the difference, but he puts so much of that sugary vanilla creamer in his mug that it's basically dessert anyway. I've told him a hundred times he needs to watch his sugar intake, especially with his family's history of diabetes, but he just brushes me off. Just like he brushes off everything I say about what his family is doing. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I don't see or hear anything that isn't really there. Everything I'm seeing with his family is very real.\n\nDoctor: Before these recent problems started, did you tend to distrust or question other people’s motives in most areas of your life?\n\nPatient: I feel like we kind of just talked about this, but no. I really didn't. I never used to question why people did things or what their motives were. I always just assumed everyone was going about their day, you know? \n\nLike at work—I do the billing and posting for a dental office—and when I first started there years ago, I trained under this woman named Patty. Patty was... well, she was definitely a character. She had this massive collection of ceramic frogs all over her desk. Just dozens of them staring at you while you're trying to figure out the insurance codes. I never thought she had some weird motive for it, even though it took up half the counter space and made it impossible to sort the daily invoices. We use this really outdated software system, too, and the screen always freezes right when I'm trying to post a payment. It drives me absolutely crazy. I keep telling the office manager we need to upgrade, but they won't spend the money. They won't even buy the good paper towels for the breakroom, just those rough brown ones from Uline that feel like literal sandpaper. I brought in my own roll of Bounty last week because I just couldn't take it anymore. \n\nBut anyway... no, I never used to overthink people's motives or distrust anyone. Not until his family started doing all this behind my back.\n\nDoctor: Besides your husband’s family, are there other people or groups you believe are intentionally targeting or harming you?\n\nPatient: No, it's really just them. I don't have any issues with anyone else. I mean, there's the mailman, but he's just incompetent, not out to get me. He keeps leaving our packages on the bottom step where they get soaked if it rains, instead of bringing them up to the porch. \n\nI ordered this beautiful set of mixing bowls from QVC last month—the Pioneer Woman ones, with the little floral patterns? They were on clearance, and I've been wanting them for ages because my old plastic ones are all scratched up from when I tried to use the hand mixer to mash potatoes. Which, by the way, do not do that. It makes them turn into literal glue. You have to use a ricer or just mash them by hand with a little bit of butter and whole milk. Not skim milk, skim milk is just water lying about being milk. My sister-in-law uses skim milk in her mashed potatoes, and they taste like wallpaper paste. But of course, she thinks she's a gourmet chef because she took one cooking class at the community center five years ago. \n\nBut anyway, no. It's just his family. I don't think the government or the neighbors or anyone else cares what I'm doing. It's just his family that's obsessed with ruining my life.\n\nDoctor: Have these beliefs ever caused you to behave in ways that other people would see as very unusual or hard to follow?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I still get up every day and go to work. I handle all my billing clerk duties, I keep the house running, I do the laundry. I even still have time for my hobbies, like gardening and painting. \n\nI've been working on this watercolor of a blue heron I saw down by the creek last spring. Watercolors are so tricky, though. You put down one wrong drop of water and the whole sky bleeds into the trees and looks like a muddy mess. I had to buy this special cold-pressed paper from Michael's just to get the texture right. I actually had a coupon for 40% off one regular-priced item, but the cashier tried to tell me it didn't apply to the paper because it was already on some unadvertised sale. We went back and forth for like ten minutes until the manager finally came over and just overrode the register. I don't know why they make it so difficult to just use a coupon. It's like they send them out in the mail just to trick you into coming to the store. \n\nBut anyway, no, I don't act weird or anything. The only time things get... I guess, complicated... is when I try to warn people about what my in-laws are doing. That's when people start looking at me funny, like they just don't want to hear the truth. But otherwise, my life is completely normal.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt this kind of mistrust toward many different people and relationships for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: No, I really haven't. Like I said earlier, I never had any issues trusting people before all this started. I've always just taken people at face value. \n\nMy best friend Brenda, we've known each other since we were in our twenties working at the old Woolworth's before it closed down. She's the godmother to my oldest, actually. Brenda has this habit of always running exactly fifteen minutes late to everything. If we say we're meeting for lunch at noon, I know not to even pull into the Applebee's parking lot until 12:15. We always get the same booth in the back, the one near the kitchen doors where the air conditioning vent doesn't blow right on your neck. I always order the Oriental Chicken Salad, but I ask for the dressing on the side because they absolutely drown it otherwise. Last time we went, the waitress—she looked like she was maybe sixteen, poor thing—dropped a whole tray of iced teas right next to our table. Brenda jumped up so fast she knocked her purse over and all her lipsticks rolled under the booth. She must carry ten different shades of pink in there. \n\nBut no, I've never had a problem trusting Brenda, or my coworkers, or anyone else. It's really just been this situation with his family that's made me realize you can't always trust the people who are supposed to be closest to you.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been like this with many different people, feeling that they were out to get you or could not be trusted?\n\nPatient: No, no, I just said that. I've never been like this with anyone else. It's just his family. I've always been a very trusting person, maybe too trusting sometimes. \n\nLike when I bought my first car, a little silver Honda Civic. The salesman told me the undercoating was absolutely necessary because of the road salt in the winter, and I just believed him and paid the extra four hundred dollars. My dad was so mad when he found out. He said they just spray some cheap rubberized paint under there and charge you a fortune for it. Dad always bought Fords, anyway. He had this big F-150 that he used to haul firewood. We'd go out to my uncle's property in the fall and cut up all the dead ash trees. The emerald ash borer really did a number on those trees back in the day. It's a shame, really, because ash wood burns so nice and hot in a woodstove. We had a woodstove in the basement when I was growing up, and my mom would dry our winter mittens on a rack next to it. They always smelled a little bit like smoke, but it was a good smell. \n\nBut anyway, no. I don't go around thinking everyone is out to get me. It's just them.\n\nDoctor: Before these recent problems started, when you were a young adult, did you already tend to assume that other people might be exploiting or deceiving you?\n\nPatient: No, I really didn't. Like I said, I was always pretty trusting. I mean, when I was in my twenties, I was just focused on working and paying my rent, not worrying about people trying to trick me. \n\nMy first apartment was this tiny one-bedroom over a bakery. Oh, the smell of that place at four in the morning! They made these incredible cinnamon rolls with the really thick cream cheese frosting. The baker, Mr. Rossi, he used to give me the day-old ones for free. He was such a sweet man, always wore this flour-dusted apron that looked like it hadn't been washed in a week. He had this big orange cat named Marmalade that used to sleep right on top of the display case. You probably couldn't get away with that now, with the health department and all. They're so strict these days. Did you know they made the little diner down the street from me put in a whole new three-compartment sink just because the old one was an inch too shallow? It cost them thousands of dollars. \n\nBut yeah, back then, I never thought people were out to get me. I just assumed everyone was basically decent, you know?\n\nDoctor: Have you always had a hard time getting close to people because you worry they may use or betray you?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. Like I keep saying, I've never had trouble getting close to people or thought they were going to use me. I've always made friends pretty easily. \n\nWhen I first started at the medical billing office, oh, it must be fifteen years ago now, I became best friends with the girl at the desk next to me, Diane. We were so close we'd even coordinate our outfits on Fridays. They used to let us wear jeans on Fridays if we put a dollar in the charity fund. The charity jar was this old plastic pretzel tub that our office manager, Susan, brought in from home. Susan absolutely loved those peanut butter filled pretzels. She'd buy the massive tubs from Costco and just sit there crunching on them all afternoon while she was running the aging reports. \n\nIt drove me a little crazy, honestly, the crunching sound. I have this thing about chewing noises. My ex-husband used to chew ice cubes after he finished his soda, just sit there on the couch going *crunch, crunch, crunch* while we were trying to watch Jeopardy. He was terrible at Jeopardy, by the way. He'd always guess the most ridiculous things before Alex Trebek even finished reading the clue. We used to watch it every night at 7:30 right after the local news. The local news anchors back then were so much better than the ones they have now. Now they all look like they just graduated from high school and half the time they can't even read the teleprompter right. \n\nBut anyway, no. I've never been afraid to get close to people. It's really just his family that I know I can't trust.\n\nDoctor: Before these recent problems started, did you ever strongly believe that other people were talking about you or plotting against you?\n\nPatient: No, never. I mean, before all this started with his family, I never thought anyone was talking about me or plotting anything. I just went about my business. Like, I'd go to work, come home, maybe stop at the grocery store. \n\nI used to go to the Stop & Shop over on Route 9, the one with the really big floral department. They always had the most beautiful tulips in the spring. I'd buy a bunch every Friday just to brighten up the kitchen table. My grandmother used to grow tulips, actually. She had this huge garden in her backyard, right next to the chain-link fence. She had these bright red ones that she said came all the way from Holland. I don't know if that was true, but she was so proud of them. She'd spend hours out there on her hands and knees, pulling weeds. She had these awful floral gardening gloves that were always caked in mud. She lived to be ninety-two, you know. Never took a single pill in her life, just drank a glass of warm water with lemon every morning and swore by it. \n\nI tried doing that for a while, the lemon water thing, but it just gave me terrible heartburn. I have to be so careful with acidic foods now. Even tomato sauce gets to me if I eat it too late at night. Which is a shame, because I make a really good baked ziti. The trick is to mix a little bit of ricotta cheese right into the sauce before you bake it, so it doesn't dry out. \n\nBut no, to answer your question, I never worried about people talking behind my back or anything like that. That's why this whole thing with his family has been such a shock to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever, outside of this current situation with your husband’s family, had a long-term pattern of being overly suspicious of other people or believing they were out to get you?\n\nPatient: No, honestly, I never was. Like I said, I've just never been that kind of person. I always just took people at face value, you know? If someone said they were going to do something, I believed them. \n\nLike my old neighbor, Mr. Henderson. He lived next door to me when I had that apartment over the bakery. He told me he was going to fix the loose handrail on our shared front porch, and he did it the very next day. He was a retired carpenter, actually. He had this whole workshop set up in his garage, filled with all these antique hand tools. He showed me this one saw that he said belonged to his great-grandfather. It had this beautiful carved wooden handle, mahogany, I think he said. He used to make these little wooden birdhouses and just give them away to the neighborhood kids. He painted them all different colors—bright blue, yellow, fire-engine red. \n\nI hung the red one he gave me right outside my kitchen window. I loved watching the little chickadees come to it in the winter. They're such brave little birds, staying around when it gets so cold. I used to buy that special seed mix with the black oil sunflower seeds, the kind that's supposed to give them extra energy for the winter. It's so expensive though! A small bag at the hardware store was almost fifteen dollars. Hardware stores are just a rip-off in general, though, aren't they? I went in last week just to get a replacement filter for my vacuum cleaner—it's a Hoover, one of those bagless ones—and they wanted twenty-five dollars for a little piece of foam! I ended up just washing the old one in the sink with some Dawn dish soap and letting it air dry. Works just as well. \n\nBut anyway, no, I've never been the suspicious type. This whole nightmare with his family is completely new to me. I've never dealt with anything like this before."
